From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

1971 Bangladesh–Pakistan armed conflict

**Bangladesh Liberation War**

| Part of the [independence of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Bangladesh "Independence of Bangladesh"), the [Indo-Pakistani conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_wars_and_conflicts "Indo-Pakistani wars and conflicts"), and the [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War") |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/BangladeshLiberationWarMontage.jpg/330px-BangladeshLiberationWarMontage.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:BangladeshLiberationWarMontage.jpg)<br>**Clockwise from top left**: [Martyred Intellectuals Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyred_Intellectuals_Memorial "Martyred Intellectuals Memorial"); [Bangladesh Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini "Mukti Bahini") [howitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QF_3.7-inch_mountain_howitzer "QF 3.7-inch mountain howitzer"); [A. A. K. Niazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._A._K._Niazi "A. A. K. Niazi") signs the [Pakistani Instrument of Surrender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender "Pakistani Instrument of Surrender") to the joint forces in the presence of [Jagjit Singh Aurora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagjit_Singh_Aurora "Jagjit Singh Aurora");[&#8202;](#ref-back-1-0)[\[1\]](#refnote-1) and the [PNS _Ghazi_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PNS_Ghazi "PNS Ghazi") |  |  |
|  | Date | - 26 March – 16 December 1971<br>- (8months, 2weeks and 6days) |
| Location | [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan "East Pakistan") ( [present-day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Bangladesh_after_independence "History of Bangladesh after independence") [Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh "Bangladesh"))<br>Spillovers:<br>- _Eastern Front:_ <br>  - [India–East Pakistan border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh%E2%80%93India_border "Bangladesh–India border")<br>  - [India–East Pakistan enclaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Bangladesh_enclaves "India–Bangladesh enclaves")<br>  - parts of [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India "East India") and [Northeast India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India "Northeast India")<br>  - [Bay of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Bengal "Bay of Bengal")<br>- _Western Front:_ <br>  - [India–West Pakistan border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_border "Indo-Pakistani border") <br>    - [Line of Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Control "Line of Control") ( [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir "Kashmir"))<br>    - [Zero Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero_Point_railway_station "Zero Point railway station")<br>  - [Arabian Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Sea "Arabian Sea") |  |
| Result | Bangladeshi-Indian victory[&#8202;](#ref-back-Shamshad2017-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-Shamshad2017)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lu2018-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-Lu2018)[&#8202;](#ref-back-KaulJha2018-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-KaulJha2018)<br>- [Surrender of Pakistani forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender "Pakistani Instrument of Surrender")<br>- [Independence of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Bangladesh "Independence of Bangladesh")<br>- [Military Officer's Revolt in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Pakistan_Military_Officer's_Revolt "1971 Pakistan Military Officer's Revolt") |  |
| Territorial<br>changes | East Pakistan [secedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secession "Secession") from [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan "Pakistan") as the [People's Republic of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Republic_of_Bangladesh "People's Republic of Bangladesh") |  |
| Belligerents |  |  |

The **Bangladesh Liberation War**[&#8202;](#ref-back-20-0)[\[note 2\]](#refnote-20) ( [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language "Bengali language"): মুক্তিযুদ্ধ, pronounced[\[mukt̪iɟud̪d̪ʱo\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Bengali "Help:IPA/Bengali")), also known as the **Bangladesh War of Independence**, was an [armed conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War "War") sparked by the rise of the [Bengali nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_nationalism "Bengali nationalism") and [self-determination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-determination "Self-determination") movement in [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan "East Pakistan"), which resulted in the independence of [Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh "Bangladesh") with the help of [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India "India"). The war began when the Pakistani [military junta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship "Military dictatorship") based in [West Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Pakistan "West Pakistan")—under the orders of [Yahya Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_Khan "Yahya Khan")—launched [Operation Searchlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Searchlight "Operation Searchlight") against East Pakistanis on the night of 25 March 1971, initiating the [Bangladesh genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_genocide "Bangladesh genocide").[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-21)

In response to the violence, members of the [Mukti Bahini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini "Mukti Bahini")—a [guerrilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla_warfare "Guerrilla warfare") [resistance movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resistance_movement "Resistance movement") formed by Bengali military, paramilitary and civilians—launched a mass guerrilla war against the [Pakistani military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Armed_Forces "Pakistan Armed Forces"), liberating numerous towns and cities in the war's initial months. At first, the Pakistan Army regained momentum during the [monsoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsoon "Monsoon"), but Bengali [guerrillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrillas "Guerrillas") counterattacked by carrying out widespread sabotage, including through [Operation Jackpot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Jackpot "Operation Jackpot") against the [Pakistan Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Navy "Pakistan Navy"), while the nascent [Bangladesh Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Air_Force "Bangladesh Air Force") flew [sorties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sortie "Sortie") against Pakistani military bases.[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-22) India joined the war on 3 December 1971 in support of the Mukti Bahini, after Pakistan launched [preemptive air strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Chengiz_Khan "Operation Chengiz Khan") on northern India and massive flight of some 10 million East Pakistani Bengals[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-23) to the Indian state of West Bengal, following waves of emigration since Partition in 1947. The subsequent [Indo-Pakistani War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_war_of_1971 "Indo-Pakistani war of 1971") involved fighting on two fronts; with [air supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_supremacy "Air supremacy") achieved in the eastern theater and the rapid advance of the [Allied Forces of Mukti Bahini and the Indian military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitro_Bahini_order_of_battle "Mitro Bahini order of battle"), Pakistan [surrendered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender "Pakistani Instrument of Surrender") in Dhaka on 16 December 1971, in what remains to date the largest surrender of armed personnel since the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War "Second World War").[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-24)

Rural and urban areas across East Pakistan saw extensive military operations and air strikes to suppress the tide of [civil disobedience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-cooperation_movement_(1971) "Non-cooperation movement (1971)") that formed after the [1970 election stalemate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_Pakistani_general_election "1970 Pakistani general election"). The [Pakistan Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Army "Pakistan Army"), backed by Islamists, created radical religious militias—the [Razakars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Razakar_(Pakistan) "Razakar (Pakistan)"), [Al-Badr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Badr_(East_Pakistan) "Al-Badr (East Pakistan)") and [Al-Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shams_(East_Pakistan) "Al-Shams (East Pakistan)")—to assist it during raids on the local populace.[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-25)[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-26)[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-27)[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-28)[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-29) Members of the Pakistani military and supporting militias engaged in mass murder, deportation and [genocidal rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War "Rape during the Bangladesh Liberation War"), pursuing a systematic campaign of annihilation against nationalist Bengali civilians, students, [intelligentsia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligentsia "Intelligentsia"), religious minorities and armed personnel. The capital, [Dhaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaka "Dhaka"), was the scene of numerous massacres, including the [Dhaka University massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Dhaka_University_massacre "1971 Dhaka University massacre"). [Sectarian violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarian_violence "Sectarian violence") also broke out between Bengalis and [Urdu-speaking Biharis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Biharis_in_Bangladesh "Persecution of Biharis in Bangladesh"). An estimated 10million Bengali [refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Bengali_refugees#1970s "East Bengali refugees") fled to neighboring India, while 30million were internally displaced.[&#8202;](#ref-back-30-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-30)

The war changed the geopolitical landscape of South Asia, with the emergence of Bangladesh as the world's seventh-most populous country. Due to complex regional alliances, the war was a major episode in [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War") tensions involving the United States, the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") and China. The majority of member states in the United Nations recognized Bangladesh as a sovereign nation in 1972.

## Background

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/India_religion_map_1909_en.jpg/330px-India_religion_map_1909_en.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:India_religion_map_1909_en.jpg) A map of the [British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj "British Raj") in 1909 showing [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim "Muslim") majority areas in green, including modern-day Bangladesh in the east and Pakistan in the west

Before the [Partition of British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India "Partition of India"), the [Lahore Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_Resolution "Lahore Resolution") initially envisaged separate [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim "Muslim")-majority states in British India's eastern and northwestern zones. A proposal for an independent [United Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Bengal "United Bengal") was mooted by Prime Minister [Huseyn Shaheed Suhrawardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huseyn_Shaheed_Suhrawardy "Huseyn Shaheed Suhrawardy") in 1946 but opposed by the colonial authorities. The [East Pakistan Renaissance Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan_Renaissance_Society "East Pakistan Renaissance Society") advocated the creation of a [sovereign state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_state "Sovereign state") in eastern British India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-31-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-31)

Political negotiations led, in August 1947, to the official birth of two states, [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan "Pakistan") and India,[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-32) giving presumably permanent homes for Muslims and Hindus, respectively, after the British departed. The [Dominion of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_Pakistan "Dominion of Pakistan") comprised two geographically and culturally separate areas to the east and the west, with India in between.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sarasota_Herald-Tribune-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-Sarasota_Herald-Tribune)

The western zone was popularly (and, for a period, also officially) termed West Pakistan and the eastern zone (modern-day Bangladesh) was initially termed [East Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Bengal "East Bengal") and later East Pakistan. Although the two zones' population was close to equal, political power was concentrated in West Pakistan, and it was widely perceived that East Pakistan was being exploited economically, leading to many grievances. Administration of two non- [contiguous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographic_contiguity "Geographic contiguity") territories was also seen as a challenge.[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-34)

On 25 March 1971, after an election won by an East Pakistani political party (the [Awami League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Awami_League "Bangladesh Awami League")) was ignored by the ruling (West Pakistani) establishment, rising political discontent and [cultural nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Types_of_nationalism#Cultural_nationalism "Types of nationalism") in East Pakistan was met by brutal[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-35) and suppressive force from the ruling elite of the West Pakistan establishment, in what came to be termed [Operation Searchlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Searchlight "Operation Searchlight").[&#8202;](#ref-back-epw-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-epw) The Pakistan Army's violent crackdown[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-37) led Awami League leader [Sheikh Mujibur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman "Sheikh Mujibur Rahman") to [declare independence of East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_Bangladeshi_Independence "Proclamation of Bangladeshi Independence") as the sovereign state of Bangladesh on 26 March 1971.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-:1) Most [Bengalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengalis "Bengalis") supported this move, although some [Islamists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamism "Islamism") and Biharis opposed it and sided with the Pakistan Army instead.[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-39)

Pakistani president [Agha Muhammad Yahya Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_Khan "Yahya Khan") ordered the Pakistani military to restore the Pakistani government's authority, beginning the civil war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[36\]](#refnote-:1) The war led a substantial number of refugees (estimated at the time to be about 10million)[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-40)[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-41) to flood [India's eastern provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India "East India").[&#8202;](#ref-back-report-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-report) Facing a mounting humanitarian and economic crisis, India actively aided and organized the Bangladeshi resistance army, the [Mukti Bahini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini "Mukti Bahini").\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### Language controversy

Main article: [Bengali language movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language_movement "Bengali language movement")

In 1948, [Governor-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Pakistan "Governor-General of Pakistan") [Muhammad Ali Jinnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_Jinnah "Muhammad Ali Jinnah") declared that " [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu "Urdu"), and only Urdu" would be Pakistan's federal language.[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-43)[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-44) But Urdu was historically prevalent only in the north, central, and western [subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent "Indian subcontinent"); in East Bengal, the native language was [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language "Bengali language"), one of the two most easterly branches of the [Indo-European languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages "Indo-European languages").[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-45) Bengali speakers constituted over 56% of Pakistan's population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-46)[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-47)

The government stand was widely viewed as an attempt to suppress the culture of the eastern wing. The people of East Bengal demanded that their language be given federal status alongside Urdu and English. The [Language Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language_movement "Bengali language movement") began in 1948, as civil society protested the removal of [Bengali script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_alphabet "Bengali alphabet") from currency and stamps, which were in place since the [British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj "British Raj").[&#8202;](#ref-back-International_Mother_Language_Day-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-International_Mother_Language_Day)

The movement reached its climax in 1952, when on 21 February, the police fired on protesting students and civilians, causing several deaths. The day is revered in Bangladesh as the [Language Movement Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_Movement_Day "Language Movement Day"). In memory of the deaths, [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO "UNESCO") declared 21 February [International Mother Language Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Mother_Language_Day "International Mother Language Day") in November 1999.[&#8202;](#ref-back-International_Mother_Language_Day-1)[\[46\]](#refnote-International_Mother_Language_Day)

### Disparities

Although East Pakistan had the larger population, West Pakistan dominated the divided country politically and received more money from the common budget.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

| Year | Spending on West Pakistan (in millions of [Pakistani rupees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_rupee "Pakistani rupee")) | Spending on East Pakistan (in millions of Pakistani rupees) | Amount spent on East as percentage of West |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1950–55 | 11,290 | 5,240 | 46.4 |
| 1955–60 | 16,550 | 5,240 | 31.7 |
| 1960–65 | 33,550 | 14,040 | 41.8 |
| 1965–70 | 51,950 | 21,410 | 41.2 |
| **Total** | **113,340** | **45,930** | **40.5** |
| _Source: Reports of the Advisory Panels for the Fourth Five Year Plan 1970–75, Vol. I,_<br>_published by the planning commission of Pakistan._ |  |  |  |

East Pakistan was already economically disadvantaged at the time of Pakistan's creation yet this economic disparity only increased under Pakistani rule. Factors included not only the deliberate state discrimination in developmental policies but also the fact that the presence of the country's capital and more immigrant businessmen in the Western Wing directed greater government allocations there. Due to low numbers of native businessmen in East Pakistan, substantial labor unrest and a tense political environment, there were also much lower foreign investments in the eastern wing. The Pakistani state's economic outlook was geared towards urban industry, which was not compatible with East Pakistan's mainly agrarian economy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Willem_van_Schendel_136-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-Willem_van_Schendel_136)

Also, Bengalis were underrepresented in the Pakistani military. Officers of Bengali origin in the different wings of the armed forces made up just 5% of the overall force by 1965; of these, only a few were in command positions, with the majority in technical or administrative posts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Library-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-Library) West Pakistanis believed that Bengalis were not "martially inclined", unlike [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns "Pashtuns") and [Punjabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabis "Punjabis"); Bengalis dismissed the " [martial races](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_race "Martial race")" notion as ridiculous and humiliating.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Library-1)[\[48\]](#refnote-Library)

Moreover, despite huge defense spending, East Pakistan received none of the benefits, such as contracts, purchasing and military support jobs. The [Indo-Pakistani War of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1965 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1965") over [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir "Kashmir") also highlighted the sense of military insecurity among Bengalis, as only an under-strength [infantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infantry "Infantry") division and 15 [combat aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_aircraft#Combat_aircraft "Military aircraft") without tank support were in East Pakistan to repulse any Indian retaliations during the conflict.[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-51)[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-52)

### Ideological and cultural differences

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Shaheed_minar_Roehl.jpg/250px-Shaheed_minar_Roehl.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Shaheed_minar_Roehl.jpg) Language movement memorial

In 1947, the Bengali Muslims had identified themselves with Pakistan's Islamic project, but by the 1970s, the people of East Pakistan had given priority to their Bengali ethnicity over their religious identity, desiring a society in accordance with Western principles such as [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism "Secularism"), democracy and socialism.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Willem_van_Schendel_183-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-Willem_van_Schendel_183) Many Bengali Muslims strongly objected to the Islamist paradigm the Pakistani state imposed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Haqqani2010-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-Haqqani2010)

Most members of West Pakistan's ruling elite shared a vision of a liberal society, but nevertheless viewed a common faith as an essential mobilizing factor behind Pakistan's creation and the subsuming of Pakistan's multiple regional identities into one national identity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Haqqani2010-1)[\[52\]](#refnote-Haqqani2010) West Pakistanis were substantially more supportive than East Pakistanis of an Islamic state, a tendency that persisted after 1971.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Baxter_1997_70-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-Baxter_1997_70)

Cultural and linguistic differences between the two wings gradually outweighed any sense of religious unity. The Bengalis took great pride in their culture and language which, with its [Bengali script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_alphabet "Bengali alphabet") and [vocabulary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_vocabulary "Bengali vocabulary"), was unacceptable to the West Pakistani elite, who believed that it had assimilated considerable Hindu cultural influences.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Willem_van_Schendel_183-1)[\[51\]](#refnote-Willem_van_Schendel_183)[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-56) West Pakistanis, in an attempt to "Islamise" the East, wanted the Bengalis to adopt Urdu.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Willem_van_Schendel_183-2)[\[51\]](#refnote-Willem_van_Schendel_183) The activities of the language movement nurtured a sentiment among Bengalis in favor of discarding Pakistan's communalism in favor of secular politics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-57) The Awami League began propagating its secular message through its newspaper to the Bengali readership.[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-58)

The Awami League's emphasis on secularism differentiated it from the Muslim League.[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-59) In 1971, the Bangladeshi liberation struggle against Pakistan was led by secular leaders[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-:0) and secularists hailed the Bangladeshi victory as the triumph of secular Bengali nationalism over religion-centred Pakistani nationalism.[&#8202;](#ref-back-RiazRahman2016-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-RiazRahman2016) While Pakistan's government strives for an Islamic state, Bangladesh was established secular.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Baxter_1997_70-1)[\[53\]](#refnote-Baxter_1997_70) After the liberation victory, the Awami League attempted to build a secular order[&#8202;](#ref-back-Baxter2018-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-Baxter2018) and the pro-Pakistan Islamist parties were barred from political participation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-63) The majority of East Pakistani [ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama "Ulama") had either remained neutral or supported the Pakistani state, since they felt that the breakup of Pakistan would be detrimental for Islam.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ahmed1998-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-Ahmed1998)

### Political differences

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman_in_1950.jpg/250px-Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman_in_1950.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman_in_1950.jpg) [Sheikh Mujibur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman "Sheikh Mujibur Rahman"), the leader of East Pakistan, and later Bangladesh

Although East Pakistan accounted for a slight majority of the country's population,[&#8202;](#ref-back-pop-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-pop) political power remained in the hands of West Pakistanis. Since a straightforward system of representation based on population would have concentrated political power in East Pakistan, the West Pakistani establishment came up with the " [One Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Unit "One Unit")" scheme, whereby all of West Pakistan was considered one province. This was solely to counterbalance the East wing's votes.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

After the 1951 assassination of [Liaquat Ali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liaquat_Ali_Khan "Liaquat Ali Khan"), Pakistan's first prime minister, political power began to devolve to the new position of [President of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Pakistan "President of Pakistan"), which replaced the office of [Governor General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Pakistan "Governor-General of Pakistan") when Pakistan became a republic, and, eventually, the military. The nominal elected chief executive, the Prime Minister, was frequently sacked by the establishment, acting through the President.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

The East Pakistanis observed that the West Pakistani establishment swiftly deposed any East Pakistanis elected leader of Pakistan, such as [Khawaja Nazimuddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khawaja_Nazimuddin "Khawaja Nazimuddin"), [Mohammad Ali Bogra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Ali_Bogra "Mohammad Ali Bogra"), [Huseyn Shaheed Suhrawardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huseyn_Shaheed_Suhrawardy "Huseyn Shaheed Suhrawardy"), and [Iskander Mirza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iskander_Mirza "Iskander Mirza"). Their suspicions were further aggravated by the military dictatorships of [Ayub Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayub_Khan_(general) "Ayub Khan (general)") (27 October 1958 – 25 March 1969) and [Yahya Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_Khan "Yahya Khan") (25 March 1969 – 20 December 1971), both West Pakistanis. The situation reached a climax in 1970, when the [Bangladesh Awami League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Awami_League "Bangladesh Awami League"), the largest East Pakistani political party, led by [Sheikh Mujibur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman "Sheikh Mujibur Rahman"), won a landslide victory in the national elections. The party won 167 of the 169 seats allotted to East Pakistan, and thus a majority of the 313 seats in the National Assembly. This gave the Awami League the constitutional right to form a government. However, [Zulfikar Ali Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulfikar_Ali_Bhutto "Zulfikar Ali Bhutto") (a former foreign minister), the leader of the [Pakistan People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_People's_Party "Pakistan People's Party"), refused to allow Rahman to become the prime minister of Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hassan_2000_p._393-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-Hassan_2000_p._393)

Instead, he proposed the idea of having two prime ministers, one for each wing. The proposal elicited outrage in the east wing, already chafing under the other constitutional innovation, the "One Unit scheme". Bhutto also refused to accept Rahman's [Six Points](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Six_point_movement "Six point movement"). On 3 March 1971, the two leaders of the two wings along with the president general Yahya Khan met in [Dacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaka "Dhaka") to decide the fate of the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hassan_2000_p._393-1)[\[64\]](#refnote-Hassan_2000_p._393)

After their discussions yielded no satisfactory results, Sheikh Mujibur Rahman called for a [nationwide strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-cooperation_movement_(1971) "Non-cooperation movement (1971)"). Bhutto feared a civil war, therefore, he sent his trusted companion, [Mubashir Hassan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mubashir_Hassan "Mubashir Hassan").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hassan_2000_p._393-2)[\[64\]](#refnote-Hassan_2000_p._393) A message was conveyed, and Rahman decided to meet Bhutto.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hassan_2000_p._393-3)[\[64\]](#refnote-Hassan_2000_p._393) Upon his arrival, Rahman met with Bhutto and both agreed to form a coalition government with Rahman as premier and Bhutto as president,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hassan_2000_p._393-4)[\[64\]](#refnote-Hassan_2000_p._393) but Sheikh Mujib later ruled out such a possibility.[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-67) Meanwhile, the military was unaware of these developments, and Bhutto increased his pressure on Rahman to reach a decision.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hassan_2000_p._393-5)[\[64\]](#refnote-Hassan_2000_p._393)

Between 10 and 13 March, [Pakistan International Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_International_Airlines "Pakistan International Airlines") canceled all its international routes to urgently fly "government passengers" to Dacca. These "government passengers" were almost all Pakistani soldiers in civilian dress. MV _Swat_, a ship of the Pakistan Navy carrying ammunition and soldiers, was harbored in [Chittagong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chittagong "Chittagong") Port, but the Bengali workers and sailors at the port refused to unload the ship. A unit of [East Pakistan Rifles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guards_Bangladesh "Border Guards Bangladesh") refused to obey commands to fire on the Bengali demonstrators, beginning a mutiny among the Bengali soldiers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-68)[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-69)[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-70)

### Response to the 1970 cyclone and Bhashani's role

Main article: [1970 Bhola cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_Bhola_cyclone "1970 Bhola cyclone")

The 1970 Bhola cyclone made [landfall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landfall "Landfall") on the East Pakistan coastline during the evening of 12 November, around the same time as a local [high tide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tide "Tide"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-IMD-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-IMD) killing an estimated 300,000 people. A 2017 [World Meteorological Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Meteorological_Organization "World Meteorological Organization") panel considers it the deadliest [tropical cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_cyclone "Tropical cyclone") since at least 1873.[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-72) A week after the landfall, President Khan conceded that his government had made "slips" and "mistakes" in its handling of the relief efforts due to a lack of understanding of the magnitude of the disaster.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-NYT)

A statement released by eleven political leaders in East Pakistan ten days after the cyclone hit charged the government with "gross neglect, callous and utter indifference". They also accused the president of playing down the magnitude of the problem in news coverage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-1)[\[72\]](#refnote-NYT) On 19 November, students held a march in Dacca protesting the slowness of the government's response.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-2)[\[73\]](#refnote-NYT)

[Abdul Hamid Khan Bhashani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hamid_Khan_Bhashani "Abdul Hamid Khan Bhashani"), a prominent politician who previously led multiple rebellions against the [British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj "British Raj"), addressed a rally of 50,000 people on 23 November 1970, where he accused the president of inefficiency and demanded his resignation. He was the first ever Bengali to declare independence of East Pakistan during this rally but it was not officially recognized.[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-76)[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-77)

As the conflict between East and West Pakistan developed in March, the Dacca offices of the two government organizations directly involved in relief efforts were closed for at least two weeks, first by a [general strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_strike "General strike") and then by a ban on government work in East Pakistan by the [Awami League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Awami_League "Bangladesh Awami League"). With this increase in tension, foreign personnel were evacuated over fears of violence. Relief work continued in the field, but long-term planning was curtailed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-3)[\[76\]](#refnote-NYT) This conflict widened into the Bangladesh Liberation War in December and concluded with the creation of Bangladesh.[&#8202;](#ref-back-USAID-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-USAID)

### Operation Searchlight

Main article: [Operation Searchlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Searchlight "Operation Searchlight")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/March71.PNG/250px-March71.PNG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:March71.PNG) The location of Bengali and Pakistani military units during [Operation Searchlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Searchlight "Operation Searchlight"), March 1971

A planned military pacification carried out by the [Pakistan Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Army "Pakistan Army")—codenamed _Operation Searchlight_—started on 25 March 1971 to curb the [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_nationalism "Bengali nationalism") independence movement[&#8202;](#ref-back-epw-1)[\[34\]](#refnote-epw) by taking control of the major cities on 26 March, and then eliminating all opposition, political or military,[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-80) within one month. The Pakistani state used [anti-Bihari violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Biharis_in_Bangladesh "Persecution of Biharis in Bangladesh") by Bengalis in early March to justify Operation Searchlight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-81)

Before the beginning of the operation, all foreign journalists were systematically deported from East Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-82)

The main phase of Operation Searchlight ended with the fall of the last major town in Bengali hands in mid-May. The operation also began the [Bangladesh genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_genocide "Bangladesh genocide"). These systematic killings served only to enrage the Bengalis, resulting in East Pakistan's secession later that year. Bangladeshi media and reference books in English have published casualty figures that vary greatly, from 5,000 to 35,000 in Dacca, and 300,000 to 3,000,000 for Bangladesh as a whole.[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-83) Independent researchers, including the [British Medical Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_BMJ "The BMJ"), have put forward figures ranging from 125,000 to 505,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-84) American [political scientist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_science "Political science") [Rudolph Rummel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolph_Rummel "Rudolph Rummel") puts total deaths at 1.5million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-85) The atrocities have been called acts of [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide#International_law "Genocide").[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-86)

According to the _Asia Times:_[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-87)

> At a meeting of the military top brass, Yahya Khan declared: "Kill 3 million of them and the rest will eat out of our hands". Accordingly, on the night of 25 March, the Pakistani Army launched _Operation Searchlight_ to "crush" Bengali resistance in which Bengali members of military services were disarmed and killed, students and the intelligentsia systematically liquidated and able-bodied Bengali males just picked up and gunned down.

Although the violence focused on the provincial capital, Dacca, it also affected all parts of East Pakistan. Residential halls of the [University of Dacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Dhaka "University of Dhaka") were particularly targeted. The only Hindu residential hall— [Jagannath Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagannath_Hall "Jagannath Hall")—was destroyed by the Pakistani armed forces, and an estimated 600 to 700 of its residents were murdered. The Pakistani army denied any cold-blooded killings at the university, but the [Hamoodur Rahman Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamoodur_Rahman_Commission "Hamoodur Rahman Commission") in Pakistan concluded that overwhelming force was used. This fact, and the massacre at Jagannath Hall and nearby student dormitories of Dacca University, are corroborated by a videotape secretly filmed by Professor Nurul Ula of the [East Pakistan University of Engineering and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_University_of_Engineering_and_Technology "Bangladesh University of Engineering and Technology"), whose residence was directly opposite the student dormitories.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nurullah-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-nurullah)

The scale of the atrocities was first made clear in the West, when [Anthony Mascarenhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Mascarenhas "Anthony Mascarenhas"), a Pakistani journalist who had been sent to the province by the military authorities to write a story favorable to Pakistan, instead fled to the United Kingdom and, on 13 June 1971, published an article in _[The Sunday Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sunday_Times "The Sunday Times")_ describing the systematic killings by the military. The [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") wrote: "There is little doubt that Mascarenhas' reportage played its part in ending the war. It helped turn world opinion against Pakistan and encouraged India to play a decisive role", with Indian prime minister [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi "Indira Gandhi") saying that Mascarenhas' article led her "to prepare the ground for India's armed intervention".[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-BBC)

Sheikh Mujibur Rahman was arrested by the Pakistani Army. Yahya Khan appointed Brigadier (later General) [Rahimuddin Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahimuddin_Khan "Rahimuddin Khan") to preside over a special tribunal prosecuting Rahman with multiple charges. The tribunal's sentence was never made public, but Yahya caused the verdict to be held in abeyance in any case. Other Awami League leaders were arrested as well, while a few fled Dacca to avoid arrest. The Awami League was banned by General Yahya Khan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-90)

### Declaration of independence

See also: [Proclamation of Bangladeshi Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_Bangladeshi_Independence "Proclamation of Bangladeshi Independence") and [7 March Speech of Bangabandhu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_March_Speech_of_Bangabandhu "7 March Speech of Bangabandhu")

The violence unleashed by the Pakistani forces on 25 March 1971 proved the last straw to the efforts to negotiate a settlement. Furthermore the Pakistany army arrested Sheikh Mujibur Rahman.

A news of Sheikh Mujibur Rahman's arrest reached the radio station in [Chittagong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chittagong "Chittagong"). Major [Ziaur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziaur_Rahman "Ziaur Rahman") begun rebelling with his forces was requested to provide security for the radio station and also read the Declaration of independence on 26 March 1971.[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-91) He broadcast the announcement of the declaration of independence on behalf of Sheikh Mujibur Rahman:

> This is Swadhin Bangla Betar Kendra. I, Major Ziaur Rahman, at the direction of Bangobondhu Sheikh Mujibur Rahman, hereby declare that Independent People's Republic of Bangladesh has been established. At his direction, I have taken the command as the temporary Head of the Republic. In the name of Sheikh Mujibur Rahman, I call upon all Bengalees to rise against the attack by the West Pakistani Army. We shall fight to the last to free our motherland. Victory is, by the Grace of Allah, ours. Joy Bangla.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Gupta-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-Gupta)

The [Kalurghat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalurghat "Kalurghat") Radio Station's transmission capability was limited, but the message was picked up by a Japanese ship in the [Bay of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Bengal "Bay of Bengal"). It was then re-transmitted by [Radio Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Australia "Radio Australia")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Gupta-1)[\[90\]](#refnote-Gupta) and later by the BBC.

26 March 1971 is considered the official [Independence Day of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_(Bangladesh) "Independence Day (Bangladesh)"), and the name Bangladesh was in effect henceforth. In July 1971, Indian prime minister [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi "Indira Gandhi") openly referred to the former East Pakistan as Bangladesh.[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-93)

## Liberation War

Main articles: [Mukti Bahini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini "Mukti Bahini") and [Timeline of the Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War "Timeline of the Bangladesh Liberation War")

### March–June

At first, resistance was spontaneous and disorganized, and was not expected to be prolonged.[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-94) But when the Pakistani Army cracked down upon the population, resistance grew. The [Mukti Bahini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini "Mukti Bahini") became increasingly active. The Pakistani military sought to quell them, but increasingly many Bengali soldiers defected to this underground "Bangladesh army". These Bengali units slowly merged into the Mukti Bahini and bolstered their weaponry with supplies from India. Pakistan responded by airlifting in two infantry divisions and reorganizing their forces. They also raised paramilitary forces of [Razakars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Razakar_(Pakistan) "Razakar (Pakistan)"), [Al-Badrs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Badr_(East_Pakistan) "Al-Badr (East Pakistan)") and [Al-Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shams_(East_Pakistan) "Al-Shams (East Pakistan)") (mostly members of the Muslim League and other Islamist groups), as well as other Bengalis who opposed independence, and [Bihari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bihar "Bihar") Muslims who had settled during the time of [partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India "Partition of India").\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

On 17 April 1971, a [provisional government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_Bangladesh "Provisional Government of Bangladesh") was formed in [Meherpur District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meherpur_District "Meherpur District") in western Bangladesh bordering India with Sheikh Mujibur Rahman, who was in prison in Pakistan, as president, [Syed Nazrul Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syed_Nazrul_Islam "Syed Nazrul Islam") as acting president, [Tajuddin Ahmad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajuddin_Ahmad "Tajuddin Ahmad") as prime minister, and General [Muhammad Ataul Ghani Osmani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M._A._G._Osmani "M. A. G. Osmani") as commander-in-chief, Bangladesh Forces. As fighting grew between the occupation army and the Bengali Mukti Bahini, an estimated 10million Bengalis sought refuge in the Indian states of Assam and West Bengal.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### June–September

See also: [List of sectors in the Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sectors_in_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War "List of sectors in the Bangladesh Liberation War") and [Military plans of the Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_plans_of_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War "Military plans of the Bangladesh Liberation War")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Sectors_of_Bangladesh_Liberation_War.svg/250px-Sectors_of_Bangladesh_Liberation_War.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sectors_of_Bangladesh_Liberation_War.svg) [The eleven sectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sectors_in_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War "List of sectors in the Bangladesh Liberation War") during the Bangladesh Liberation War[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/George_Harrison_-_Bangla_Desh.png/250px-George_Harrison_-_Bangla_Desh.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:George_Harrison_-_Bangla_Desh.png) An advertisement for former [Beatle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles "The Beatles") [George Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Harrison "George Harrison")'s " [Bangla Desh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangla_Desh_(song) "Bangla Desh (song)")" single, released in July 1971 to raise international awareness and funds for the millions of Bangladeshi refugees

Bangladesh forces command was set up on 11 July, with Col. [M. A. G. Osmani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M._A._G._Osmani "M. A. G. Osmani") as commander-in-chief (C-in-C) with the status of Cabinet Minister, Lt. Col. Abdur Rabb as chief of Staff (COS), Group Captain A. K. Khandker as Deputy Chief of Staff (DCOS) and Major A. R. Chowdhury as Assistant Chief of Staff (ACOS).\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

Osmani had differences of opinion with the Indian leadership about the role of the Mukti Bahini in the conflict. Indian leadership initially envisioned a well trained force of 8,000 guerrillas, operating in small cells around Bangladesh to facilitate eventual conventional combat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-95) With the Bangladesh government in exile, Osmani favored a different strategy:[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-96)[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-97)

- Bengali conventional forces would occupy lodgments inside Bangladesh and the Bangladesh government would request international [diplomatic recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_recognition "Diplomatic recognition") and intervention. Initially [Mymensingh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mymensingh "Mymensingh") was picked for this operation, but Osmani later settled on Sylhet.
- Sending the maximum number of guerrillas into Bangladesh as soon as possible with the following objectives:[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-98)[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-99)
  - Increasing Pakistani casualties through raids and ambush.
  - Cripple economic activity by hitting power stations, railway lines, storage depots and communication networks.
  - Destroy Pakistan Army mobility by blowing up bridges/culverts, fuel depots, trains and river crafts.
  - The strategic objective was to make the Pakistanis spread their forces inside the province, so attacks could be made on isolated Pakistani detachments.

Bangladesh was divided into [eleven sectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sectors_in_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War "List of sectors in the Bangladesh Liberation War") in July,[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-100) each with a commander chosen from defected officers of the Pakistani army who joined the Mukti Bahini to lead guerrilla operations.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] The Mukti Bahini forces were given two to five weeks of training by the Indian army on guerrilla warfare.[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-101) Most of their training camps were near the border area and operated with assistance from India. The 10th Sector was placed under Osmani's command and included the Naval Commandos and C-in-C's special force.[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-102) Three brigades (eventually 8 battalions) were raised for conventional warfare; a large guerrilla force (estimated at 100,000) was trained.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-103)

Five infantry battalions were reformed and positioned along the northern and eastern borders of Bangladesh. Three more battalions were raised, and artillery batteries were formed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-104) During June and July, Mukti Bahini regrouped across the border with Indian aid through [Operation Jackpot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Jackpot "Operation Jackpot") and began sending 2,000–5,000 guerrillas across the border,[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-105) the so-called Monsoon Offensive, which for various reasons (lack of proper training, supply shortage, lack of a proper support network inside Bangladesh) failed to achieve its objectives.[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-106)[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-107) Bengali regular forces also attacked border outposts in [Mymensingh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mymensingh "Mymensingh"), [Comilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comilla "Comilla") and [Sylhet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylhet "Sylhet"), but the results were mixed. Pakistani authorities concluded that they had successfully contained the Monsoon Offensive, which proved a near-accurate observation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-108)

Guerrilla operations, which slackened during the training phase, picked up after August. Economic and military targets in Dacca were attacked. The major success story was Operation Jackpot, in which naval commandos mined and blew up berthed ships in Chittagong, [Mongla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Mongla "Port of Mongla"), [Narayanganj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narayanganj "Narayanganj") and [Chandpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandpur_District "Chandpur District") on 15 August 1971.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Untold_heroism_of_Muktibahini_Frogmen-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-Untold_heroism_of_Muktibahini_Frogmen)[&#8202;](#ref-back-On_the_water_front_spectacular_achievement-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-On_the_water_front_spectacular_achievement)

### October–December

See also: [Mitro Bahini Order of Battle December 1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitro_Bahini_Order_of_Battle_December_1971 "Mitro Bahini Order of Battle December 1971"); [Pakistan Army order of battle, December 1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Army_order_of_battle,_December_1971 "Pakistan Army order of battle, December 1971"); [Evolution of Pakistan Eastern Command plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution_of_Pakistan_Eastern_Command_plan "Evolution of Pakistan Eastern Command plan"); and [Operation Jackpot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Jackpot "Operation Jackpot")

|  |
| --- |
| **Major battles**<br>- [Battle of Boyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Boyra "Battle of Boyra")<br>- [Battle of Garibpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Garibpur "Battle of Garibpur")<br>- [Battle of Dhalai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Dhalai "Battle of Dhalai")<br>- [Battle of Hilli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Hilli "Battle of Hilli")<br>- [Battle of Kushtia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kushtia "Battle of Kushtia") |

Bangladeshi conventional forces attacked border outposts. Kamalpur, Belonia and the [Battle of Boyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Boyra "Battle of Boyra") are a few examples. 90 out of 370 border outposts fell to Bengali forces. Guerrilla attacks intensified, as did Pakistani and Razakar reprisals on civilian populations. Pakistani forces were reinforced by eight battalions from West Pakistan. The Bangladeshi independence fighters even managed to temporarily capture [airstrips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerodrome#Airstrip "Aerodrome") at [Lalmonirhat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lalmonirhat_District "Lalmonirhat District") and [Shalutikar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalutikar?action=edit&redlink=1 "Shalutikar (page does not exist)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACIG-2)[\[7\]](#refnote-ACIG) Both of these were used for flying in supplies and arms from India. Pakistan sent another five battalions from West Pakistan as reinforcements.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

## Indian involvement

See also: [Indo-Pakistani war of 1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_war_of_1971 "Indo-Pakistani war of 1971")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Bangladesh_1971_Liberation.jpg/330px-Bangladesh_1971_Liberation.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bangladesh_1971_Liberation.jpg) Illustration showing military units and troop movements during the war

> All unprejudiced persons objectively surveying the grim events in Bangladesh since March 25 have recognised the revolt of 75 million people, a people who were forced to the conclusion that neither their life, nor their liberty, to say nothing of the possibility of the pursuit of happiness, was available to them.

— [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi "Indira Gandhi"), Letter to [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon "Richard Nixon"), 15 December 1971[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-111)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Indira_Gandhi_face_detail%2C_from-_Indira_Gandhi_and_the_Johnsons_at_the_North_Portico_of_the_White_House_%28cropped%29.tif/lossy-page1-250px-Indira_Gandhi_face_detail%2C_from-_Indira_Gandhi_and_the_Johnsons_at_the_North_Portico_of_the_White_House_%28cropped%29.tif.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Indira_Gandhi_face_detail,_from-_Indira_Gandhi_and_the_Johnsons_at_the_North_Portico_of_the_White_House_(cropped).tif) [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi "Indira Gandhi")

Indian prime minister Indira Gandhi concluded that instead of taking in millions of refugees, India would be economically better off going to war against Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MSN_Encarta-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-MSN_Encarta) As early as 28 April 1971, the Indian Cabinet had asked General [Manekshaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Manekshaw "Sam Manekshaw") ( [Chairman of the Chiefs of Staff Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_Chiefs_of_Staff_Committee "Chairman of the Chiefs of Staff Committee")) to "Go into East Pakistan".[&#8202;](#ref-back-113-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-113) Hostile relations in the past between India and Pakistan added to India's decision to intervene in Pakistan's civil war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MSN_Encarta-1)[\[110\]](#refnote-MSN_Encarta) Major General [J. F. R. Jacob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._F._R._Jacob "J. F. R. Jacob"), Chief of Staff of India's Eastern Command, devised and executed the brilliant capture of Dhaka, bypassing other towns on the way there which brought about the surrender of the Pakistani commander of its Eastern Forces, [A.A.K. Niazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A.A.K._Niazi "A.A.K. Niazi") in that city.

As a result, the Indian government decided to support the creation of a separate state for ethnic Bengalis by supporting the [Mukti Bahini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini "Mukti Bahini").[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-114) [RAW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_Analysis_Wing "Research and Analysis Wing") helped to organize, train and arm these insurgents. Consequently, the Mukti Bahini succeeded in harassing Pakistani military in East Pakistan, creating conditions conducive to a full-scale Indian military intervention in early December.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MSN_Encarta-2)[\[110\]](#refnote-MSN_Encarta)

The [Pakistan Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Air_Force "Pakistan Air Force") (PAF) launched [a preemptive strike on Indian Air Force bases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Chengiz_Khan "Operation Chengiz Khan") on 3 December 1971. The attack was modeled on the [Israeli Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Air_Force "Israeli Air Force")'s [Operation Focus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Focus "Operation Focus") during the [Six-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Six-Day_War "Six-Day War") and intended to neutralize the [Indian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Air_Force "Indian Air Force") planes on the ground. India saw the strike as an open act of unprovoked aggression, which marked the official start of the [Indo-Pakistani War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1971"). In response to the attack, both India and Pakistan formally acknowledged the "existence of a state of war between the two countries" even though neither government had formally issued a [declaration of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_war "Declaration of war").[&#8202;](#ref-back-time-edge-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-time-edge)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/11/T-55_tanks_in_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War.jpg/250px-T-55_tanks_in_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:T-55_tanks_in_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War.jpg) Allied Indian [T-55](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-54/55 "T-54/55") tanks on their way to Dacca

Three Indian [corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corps "Corps") were involved in the liberation of East Pakistan. They were supported by nearly three [brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigade "Brigade") of Mukti Bahini fighting alongside them, and many more who were fighting irregularly. That was far superior to the Pakistani army of three [divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_(military) "Division (military)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-116) The Indians quickly overran the country, selectively engaging or bypassing heavily defended strongholds. Pakistani forces were unable to effectively counter the Indian attack, as they had been deployed in small units around the border to counter the guerrilla attacks by the Mukti Bahini.[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-117) Unable to defend Dacca, the Pakistanis surrendered on 16 December 1971.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### Air and naval war

The Indian Air Force carried out several sorties against Pakistan, and within a week, IAF aircraft dominated the skies of East Pakistan. It [achieved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan_Air_Operations,_1971 "East Pakistan Air Operations, 1971") near-total [air supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_supremacy "Air supremacy") by the end of the first week, as the entire Pakistani air contingent in the east, PAF No.14 Squadron, was grounded because of Indian and Bangladeshi airstrikes at Tejgaon, Kurmitola, Lalmonirhat and Shamsher Nagar. [Sea Hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawker_Sea_Hawk "Hawker Sea Hawk") from the carrier [INS _Vikrant_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INS_Vikrant_(1961) "INS Vikrant (1961)") also struck Chittagong, [Barisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barisal "Barisal") and [Cox's Bazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cox's_Bazar "Cox's Bazar"), destroying the eastern wing of the [Pakistan Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Navy "Pakistan Navy") and effectively blockading the East Pakistan ports, cutting off any escape routes for the stranded Pakistani soldiers. The nascent [Bangladesh Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Navy "Bangladesh Navy") (comprising officers and sailors who defected from the Pakistani Navy) aided the Indians in the marine warfare, carrying out attacks, most notably [Operation Jackpot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Jackpot "Operation Jackpot").[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-118)\[ _[bettersourceneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\]

## Surrender and aftermath

Further information: [Pakistani Instrument of Surrender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender "Pakistani Instrument of Surrender")

See also: [Delhi Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Agreement "Delhi Agreement")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender_1971.jpg/250px-Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender_1971.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender_1971.jpg) Pakistani Instrument of Surrender[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/1971_Instrument_of_Surrender_WaPo.jpg/250px-1971_Instrument_of_Surrender_WaPo.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1971_Instrument_of_Surrender_WaPo.jpg) Signing of [Pakistani Instrument of Surrender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender "Pakistani Instrument of Surrender") by Pakistan's Lt.Gen. [A. A. K. Niazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amir_Abdullah_Khan_Niazi "Amir Abdullah Khan Niazi") and [Jagjit Singh Aurora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagjit_Singh_Aurora "Jagjit Singh Aurora") on behalf of Indian and [Bangladesh Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini "Mukti Bahini") in [Dhaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaka "Dhaka") on 16 Dec. 1971

On 16 December 1971, Lt. Gen [Amir Abdullah Khan Niazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amir_Abdullah_Khan_Niazi "Amir Abdullah Khan Niazi"), [Chief Martial Law Administrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Martial_Law_Administrator "Chief Martial Law Administrator") of [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan "East Pakistan") and commander of Pakistan Army forces in East Pakistan signed the [Instrument of Surrender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Instrument_of_Surrender "Pakistani Instrument of Surrender"). At the time of surrender only a few countries had provided [diplomatic recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_recognition_of_Bangladesh "International recognition of Bangladesh") to the new nation. Over 93,000 Pakistani troops surrendered to the Indian forces and Bangladesh Liberation forces, making it the largest surrender since [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II").[&#8202;](#ref-back-dailytimes1912005-1)[\[14\]](#refnote-dailytimes1912005)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC1971-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-BBC1971)

Bangladesh sought admission to the UN with most voting in its favor. China vetoed this as Pakistan was its key ally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-120) The United States, also a key ally of Pakistan, was one of the last nations to accord Bangladesh recognition.[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-121) To ensure a smooth transition, in 1972 the [Simla Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_Agreement "Simla Agreement") was signed between India and Pakistan. The treaty ensured that Pakistan recognized the independence of Bangladesh in exchange for the return of the Pakistani [PoWs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoner_of_war "Prisoner of war").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-:4)

India treated all the PoWs in strict accordance with the Geneva Convention, rule 1925.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-1)[\[120\]](#refnote-:4) It released more than 93,000 Pakistani PoWs in five months.[&#8202;](#ref-back-dailytimes1912005-2)[\[14\]](#refnote-dailytimes1912005) Further, as a gesture of goodwill, nearly 200 soldiers who were sought for war crimes by Bengalis were pardoned by India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-123) The accord also gave back 13,000km2 (5,019sqmi) of land that Indian troops had seized in West Pakistan during the war, though India retained a few strategic areas,[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-124) most notably [Kargil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_district "Kargil district") (which was in turn the focal point of [a war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_War "Kargil War") between the two nations in 1999). This was done as a measure of promoting "lasting peace" and acknowledged by many observers as a sign of maturity by India. But some in India[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-125) felt the treaty had been too lenient to Bhutto, who had pleaded for leniency, arguing that the fragile democracy in Pakistan would crumble if Pakistanis perceived the accord as overly harsh.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### Reaction in West Pakistan to the war

Reaction to the defeat and dismemberment of half the nation was a shocking loss to top military and civilians alike. Few had expected that they would lose the formal war in under a fortnight, and there was also unsettlement over what was perceived as a meek surrender of the army in East Pakistan. [Yahya Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_Khan "Yahya Khan")'s dictatorship collapsed and gave way to Bhutto, who took the opportunity to rise to power.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Defencejournal-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-Defencejournal)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ghazali.net-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-ghazali.net)

[General Niazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amir_Abdullah_Khan_Niazi "Amir Abdullah Khan Niazi"), who surrendered along with 93,000 troops, was viewed with suspicion and contempt upon his return to Pakistan. He was shunned and branded a traitor. The war also exposed the shortcomings of Pakistan's declared strategic doctrine that the "defence of East Pakistan lay in West Pakistan".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Defencejournal-1)[\[124\]](#refnote-Defencejournal)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ghazali.net-1)[\[125\]](#refnote-ghazali.net)

## Atrocities

Main articles: [1971 Bangladesh genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Bangladesh_genocide "1971 Bangladesh genocide"), [1971 killing of Bengali intellectuals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_killing_of_Bengali_intellectuals "1971 killing of Bengali intellectuals"), and [Rape during the Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Bangladesh_Liberation_War "Rape during the Bangladesh Liberation War")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/2/2e/Dead_bodies_of_Bengali_intellectuals%2C_14_December_1971.jpg/250px-Dead_bodies_of_Bengali_intellectuals%2C_14_December_1971.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dead_bodies_of_Bengali_intellectuals,_14_December_1971.jpg) Rayerbazar killing field photographed immediately after the war, showing dead bodies of intellectuals (image courtesy: [Rashid Talukder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Talukder "Rashid Talukder"), 1971)

During the war, there were widespread killings and other atrocities—including the displacement of civilians in Bangladesh (East Pakistan at the time) and widespread violations of human rights beginning with [Operation Searchlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Searchlight "Operation Searchlight") on 25 March 1971. Members of the [Pakistani military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Armed_Forces "Pakistan Armed Forces") and supporting paramilitary forces killed an estimated 300,000[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC-1)[\[87\]](#refnote-BBC) to 3,000,000 people[&#8202;](#ref-back-MathewWhite-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-MathewWhite) and raped between 200,000 and 400,000 [Bangladeshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladeshis "Bangladeshis") women in a systematic campaign of [genocidal rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocidal_rape "Genocidal rape").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESharlach200092–93-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESharlach200092–93)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESajjad2012225-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESajjad2012225) Pakistan's religious leaders openly supported the crime by labeling [Bengali freedom fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini "Mukti Bahini") "Hindus" and Bengali women "the booty of war".[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-131) In fact, more than 80 percent of the Bengali people were Muslims at that time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-132)

A large section of the intellectual community of Bangladesh were murdered, mostly by the [Al-Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shams_(East_Pakistan) "Al-Shams (East Pakistan)") and [Al-Badr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Badr_(East_Pakistan) "Al-Badr (East Pakistan)") forces,[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-133) at the instruction of the Pakistani Army.[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-134) Just two days before the surrender, on 14 December 1971, Pakistan Army and the Razakar militia (local collaborators) picked up at least 100 physicians, professors, writers and engineers in Dacca, and murdered them, leaving the dead bodies in a mass grave.[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-135)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Sriti_shoud.jpeg/250px-Sriti_shoud.jpeg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sriti_shoud.jpeg) Memorial for freedom fighters

Many mass graves have been discovered in Bangladesh.[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-136) The first night of war on Bengalis, which is documented in telegrams from the American consulate in Dacca to the U.S. State Department, saw indiscriminate killings of students of [Dacca University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Dhaka "University of Dhaka") and other civilians.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-:2) Numerous women were tortured, raped, and killed during the war; the exact numbers are not known and are debated. The widespread rape of Bangladeshi women led to birth of thousands of [war babies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_children "War children").[&#8202;](#ref-back-138-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-138)[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-139)[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-140)

The Pakistan Army also kept numerous Bengali women as sex slaves inside the Dacca Cantonment. Most were captured from Dacca University and private homes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-time.oct25-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-time.oct25) There was significant sectarian violence not only perpetrated and encouraged by the Pakistani army,[&#8202;](#ref-back-usconsulate_cable_march31-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-usconsulate_cable_march31) but also by Bengali nationalists against non-Bengali minorities, especially [Biharis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biharis "Biharis").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bihari_Refugees-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-Bihari_Refugees) In June 1971, Bihari representatives said that 500,000 Biharis were killed by Bengalis.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Gerlach2010-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-Gerlach2010) R. J. Rummel gives a prudent estimate of 150,000 killed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-:12)

On 16 December 2002, the [George Washington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_University "George Washington University")'s [National Security Archive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Archive "National Security Archive") published a collection of declassified documents, consisting mostly of communications between U.S. embassy officials and [United States Information Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Information_Agency "United States Information Agency") centers in Dacca and India, and officials in Washington, D.C.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-1)[\[135\]](#refnote-:2) These documents show that U.S. officials working in diplomatic institutions within Bangladesh used the terms "selective genocide"[&#8202;](#ref-back-SelectiveGenocide-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-SelectiveGenocide) and "genocide" (see [The Blood Telegram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archer_Blood#The_Blood_telegram "Archer Blood")) for information on events they had knowledge of at the time. _[Genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide "Genocide")_ is the term still used to describe the event in almost every major publication and newspaper in Bangladesh,[&#8202;](#ref-back-bangladeshobserver-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-bangladeshobserver)[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-148) although in Pakistan, the accusations against Pakistani forces continue to be disputed.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

## International reactions

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Andr%C3%A9_Malraux%2C_Pic%2C_22.jpg/250px-Andr%C3%A9_Malraux%2C_Pic%2C_22.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Andr%C3%A9_Malraux,_Pic,_22.jpg) Former French minister [André Malraux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Malraux "André Malraux") vowed to fight alongside the Mukti Bahini in the Liberation War.[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-149)[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-150)

Following Sheikh Mujibur Rahman's declaration of independence in March 1971, a worldwide campaign was undertaken by the [Provisional Government of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_Bangladesh "Provisional Government of Bangladesh") to drum up political support for the independence of East Pakistan as well as humanitarian support for the Bengali people.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

Indian prime minister Indira Gandhi provided extensive diplomatic and political support to the Bangladesh movement. She toured many countries in a bid to create awareness of the Pakistani atrocities against Bengalis. This effort was to prove vital later during the war, in framing the world's context of the war and to justify military action by India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-recog-story-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-recog-story) Also, following Pakistan's defeat, it ensured prompt recognition of the newly independent state of Bangladesh.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### United Nations

Though the United Nations condemned the human rights violations during and following Operation Searchlight, it failed to defuse the situation politically before the start of the war.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

After India entered the war, Pakistan, fearing certain defeat, made urgent appeals to the United Nations to intervene and force India to agree to a [ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceasefire "Ceasefire"). The [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council "United Nations Security Council") assembled on 4 December 1971 to discuss the hostilities in South Asia. After lengthy discussions on 7 December, the United States made a resolution for "immediate cease-fire and withdrawal of troops". While supported by the majority, the USSR vetoed the resolution twice. In light of the Pakistani atrocities against Bengalis, the United Kingdom and France abstained on the resolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-time-edge-1)[\[113\]](#refnote-time-edge)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-:3)

On 12 December, with Pakistan facing imminent defeat, the United States requested that the Security Council be reconvened. Pakistan's deputy prime minister and foreign minister, Zulfikar Ali Bhutto, was rushed to New York City to make the case for a resolution on the ceasefire. The council continued deliberations for four days. By the time proposals were finalized, Pakistan's forces in the East had surrendered and the war had ended, making the measures merely academic. Bhutto, frustrated by the failure of the resolution and the inaction of the United Nations, ripped up his speech and left the council.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-1)[\[150\]](#refnote-:3)

Most UN member nations were quick to recognize Bangladesh within months of its independence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-recog-story-1)[\[149\]](#refnote-recog-story)

### Bhutan

As the Bangladesh Liberation War approached the defeat of the Pakistan Army, the Himalayan kingdom of [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan "Bhutan") became the first state in the world to recognize the newly independent country on 6 December 1971.[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-153) [Sheikh Mujibur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman "Sheikh Mujibur Rahman"), the first [president of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Bangladesh "President of Bangladesh"), visited Bhutan to attend the coronation of [Jigme Singye Wangchuck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jigme_Singye_Wangchuck "Jigme Singye Wangchuck"), the fourth King of Bhutan in June 1974.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### US and USSR
