[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Ted_Kennedy%2C_1967_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-Ted_Kennedy%2C_1967_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ted_Kennedy,_1967_(cropped).jpg) [Senator Ted Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Kennedy "Ted Kennedy") led US congressional support for Bangladeshi independence.

The US government stood by its old ally Pakistan in terms of diplomacy and military threats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-154) US president [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon "Richard Nixon") and his National Security Advisor [Henry Kissinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Kissinger "Henry Kissinger") feared Soviet expansion into South and Southeast Asia. Pakistan was a close ally of the People's Republic of China, with which Nixon had been negotiating a _[rapprochement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93United_States_relations#Rapprochement "China–United States relations")_ and which he [intended to visit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_visit_by_Richard_Nixon_to_China "1972 visit by Richard Nixon to China") in February 1972. Nixon feared that an Indian invasion of [West Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Pakistan "West Pakistan") would mean total Soviet domination of the region, and that it would seriously undermine the global position of the United States and the regional position of United States new tacit ally, China.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Shalom-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-Shalom)[&#8202;](#ref-back-thedailystar.net-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-thedailystar.net)

To demonstrate to China the _bona fides_ of the United States as an ally, and in direct violation of the US Congress-imposed sanctions on Pakistan, Nixon sent military supplies to Pakistan and routed them through Jordan and Iran,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Shalom-1)[\[153\]](#refnote-Shalom) while also encouraging China to increase its arms supplies to Pakistan. The Nixon administration also ignored reports it received of the genocidal activities of the Pakistani Army in East Pakistan, most notably the [Blood telegram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archer_Blood#The_Blood_telegram "Archer Blood").[&#8202;](#ref-back-thedailystar.net-1)[\[154\]](#refnote-thedailystar.net)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Yahya_and_Nixon.jpg/250px-Yahya_and_Nixon.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Yahya_and_Nixon.jpg) The [Nixon administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nixon_administration "Nixon administration") was widely criticized for its close ties with the military junta led by General [Yahya Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_Khan "Yahya Khan"). American diplomats in East Pakistan expressed profound dissent in the [Blood Telegram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_Telegram "Blood Telegram").

Nixon denied getting involved in the situation, saying that it was an internal matter of Pakistan, but when Pakistan's defeat seemed certain, he sent the aircraft carrier [USS _Enterprise_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Enterprise_(CVN-65) "USS Enterprise (CVN-65)") to the [Bay of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Bengal "Bay of Bengal"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-157) a move the Indians deemed a nuclear threat. _Enterprise_ arrived on station on 11 December 1971. On 6 and 13 December, the [Soviet Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Navy "Soviet Navy") dispatched two groups of ships, armed with nuclear missiles, from [Vladivostok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladivostok "Vladivostok"); they trailed US [Task Force 74](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Task_Force_74 "Task Force 74") in the Indian Ocean from 18 December until 7 January 1972.[&#8202;](#ref-back-White2011-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-White2011)[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-159)

The Soviet Union supported Bangladesh and Indian armies, as well as the [Mukti Bahini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukti_Bahini "Mukti Bahini") during the war, recognising that the independence of Bangladesh would weaken the position of its rivals—the United States and the People's Republic of China. It gave assurances to India that if a confrontation with the U.S. or China developed, the USSR would take countermeasures. This was enshrined in the [Indo-Soviet friendship treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Soviet_Treaty_of_Friendship_and_Cooperation "Indo-Soviet Treaty of Friendship and Cooperation") signed in August 1971. The Soviets also sent a nuclear submarine to ward off the threat posed by USS _Enterprise_ in the Indian Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-160-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-160)

At the end of the war, the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact "Warsaw Pact") countries were among the first to recognize Bangladesh. The Soviet Union accorded recognition to Bangladesh on 25 January 1972.[&#8202;](#ref-back-161-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-161) The United States delayed recognition for some months, before according it on 8 April 1972.[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-162)

### China

As a long-standing ally of Pakistan, the People's Republic of China reacted with alarm to the evolving situation in East Pakistan and the prospect of India invading West Pakistan and Pakistani-controlled [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir "Kashmir"). On 10 December 1971, Nixon instructed Kissinger to ask the Chinese to move some forces toward the frontier with India. Nixon said, "Threaten to move forces or move them, Henry, that's what they must do now". Kissinger met with [Huang Hua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huang_Hua "Huang Hua"), China's permanent representative to the United Nations, later that evening.[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-163)[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-164)[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-165)

The Chinese did not respond to this encouragement, because unlike the [1962 Sino-Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_War "Sino-Indian War"), when India was caught entirely unaware, this time the Indian Army was prepared and had deployed eight mountain divisions to the Sino-Indian border to guard against such an eventuality.[&#8202;](#ref-back-time-edge-2)[\[113\]](#refnote-time-edge) China instead threw its weight behind demands for an immediate ceasefire.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

When Bangladesh applied for membership to the United Nations in 1972, China vetoed its application[&#8202;](#ref-back-oxnard-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-oxnard) because two U.N. resolutions about the repatriation of Pakistani prisoners of war and civilians had not yet been implemented.[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-167) China was also among the last countries to recognize independent Bangladesh, refusing to do so until 31 August 1975.[&#8202;](#ref-back-recog-story-2)[\[149\]](#refnote-recog-story)[&#8202;](#ref-back-oxnard-1)[\[164\]](#refnote-oxnard)

### Sri Lanka

Sri Lanka (then called [Ceylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_Ceylon "Dominion of Ceylon")) saw the partition of Pakistan as an example for itself and feared India might use its enhanced power against it in the future.[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-168):7 Despite the left-wing government of [Sirimavo Bandaranaike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirimavo_Bandaranaike "Sirimavo Bandaranaike") following a neutral non-aligned foreign policy, Sri Lanka decided to help Pakistan in the war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-169)[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-170) As Pakistani aircraft could not fly over Indian territory, they would have to take a longer route around India and so they stopped at [Bandaranaike Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandaranaike_Airport "Bandaranaike Airport") in Sri Lanka where they were refueled before flying to [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan "East Pakistan").[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-171)

### Arab world

As many [Arab countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_countries "Arab countries") were allied with both the United States and Pakistan, it was easy for Kissinger to encourage them to participate. He sent letters to both, the [king of Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_of_Jordan "Hussein of Jordan") and the [king of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Faisal "Shah Faisal"). Nixon gave permission for Jordan to send ten [F-104s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-104 "F-104") and promised to provide replacements.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Shalom-2)[\[153\]](#refnote-Shalom) According to author Martin Bowman, "Libyan [F-5s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_F-5 "Northrop F-5") were reportedly deployed to [Sargodha AFB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargodha_Airbase "Sargodha Airbase"), perhaps as a potential training unit to prepare Pakistani pilots for an influx of more F-5s from Saudi Arabia."[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pen_and_Sword,_Bowman-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-Pen_and_Sword,_Bowman)

[Libyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Libya_under_Muammar_Gaddafi#Libyan_Arab_Republic_(1969%E2%80%931977) "History of Libya under Muammar Gaddafi") dictator [Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaddafi "Gaddafi") also personally directed a strongly worded letter to Indian prime minister Indira Gandhi accusing her of aggression against Pakistan, which endeared him to all Pakistanis.[&#8202;](#ref-back-THe_News_International-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-THe_News_International)

## Notes

1. [&#8202;](#refnote-fn1)[1](#ref-back-fn1-0) [2](#ref-back-fn1-1)Cooper and Ali's figures of 365,000 Pakistan Army and 280,000 paramilitary are for the entire Pakistan force, on the west and east fronts combined, when the [Indo-Pakistani War of 1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1971") broke out.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACIG-3)[\[7\]](#refnote-ACIG) Cloughley clarifies that only a quarter of the 365,000 Pakistan Army, roughly 91,000, was in East Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-9)
2. [&#8202;](#refnote-20)[↑](#ref-back-20-0)This war is known as the Liberation War or the War of Independence in Bangladesh.[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-18) This war is also called the Civil War in Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Social_2005_p93-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-Social_2005_p93)

Refugees are still trekking into India ... telling of villages burned, residents shot, and prominent figures carried off and never heard from again. One of the more horrible revelations concerns 563 young Bengali women, some only 18, who have been held captive inside Dacca's dingy military cantonment since the first days of the fighting. Seized from Dacca University and private homes and forced into military brothels, the girls are all three to five months pregnant. The army is reported to have enlisted Bengali gynecologists to abort girls held at military installations. But for those at the Dacca cantonment it is too late for abortion.
